## More practice

Twenty-five more — the biggest set in the book, because this is the chapter the
exam leans on hardest and because almost none of the marks are in the arithmetic.
Questions 1 to 4 are set-up only, with no numbers at all; do them first, because a
test with the wrong $H_a$ is worth nothing however neatly the rest is computed.

1. For each, state $H_0$ and $H_a$, and name the tail.
   (a) A manufacturer claims its cereal boxes average 500 g; an inspector suspects
   they are underfilled.
   (b) A training programme is meant to change productivity, and management wants to
   know whether it has.
   (c) A supplier guarantees that mean impurity is at most 3 parts per million; you
   are the buyer.
   (d) Historically mean class size was 18; the dean asks whether it has changed.
   (e) A drug company claims a mean recovery time below 7 days; a regulator checks.
   (f) A machine is calibrated to 250 ml and drifts in either direction.
   (g) An economist believes a minimum wage rise raised mean hours worked.
   (h) A bank claims mean wait time is under 4 minutes; a journalist investigates.

2. In each of these set-ups, something is wrong. Identify it.
   (a) $H_0: \bar{x} = 50$ against $H_a: \bar{x} \neq 50$.
   (b) $H_0: \mu > 100$ against $H_a: \mu < 100$.
   (c) $H_0: \mu = 20$ against $H_a: \mu = 25$.
   (d) "We will test whether $\mu > 30$, and if the sample mean comes out below 30 we
   will test whether $\mu < 30$ instead."

3. Why does the equals sign always go in $H_0$? Answer by saying what would go wrong
   in the calculation if it did not.

4. A researcher collects the data, sees that $\bar{x}$ is above $\mu_0$, and then
   decides to run a one-tailed test in the upper direction.
   (a) What is the stated significance level?
   (b) What is the actual one?
   (c) Why does this matter more than it sounds like it should?

5. A machine should fill to 500 g. A sample of $n = 64$ gives $\bar{x} = 497.5$, and
   $\sigma = 8$ is known from long experience.
   (a) State the hypotheses.
   (b) Find the standard error and the test statistic.
   (c) Find the two-tailed p-value.
   (d) Conclude at $\alpha = 0.05$ and at $\alpha = 0.01$, and comment on the pair of
   answers.

6. A tutoring programme claims to raise scores above the historical mean of 100. A
   sample of $n = 16$ students gives $\bar{x} = 104$ and $s = 12$.
   (a) State the hypotheses and the tail.
   (b) Find the standard error, the test statistic and the degrees of freedom.
   (c) Compare with the critical value and conclude at $\alpha = 0.05$.
   (d) Find the p-value.

7. Repeat question 6 as a **two-tailed** test.
   (a) What is the new critical value, and what is the new p-value?
   (b) The data have not changed. Explain why the conclusion could, in principle,
   change.

8. A bottling line is set to 500 ml. A sample of $n = 49$ gives $\bar{x} = 498.2$ and
   $s = 4.9$.
   (a) Test at $\alpha = 0.05$, two-tailed, reporting $t$, $\nu$ and the critical
   value.
   (b) Find the p-value.
   (c) State the conclusion in a sentence a plant manager would act on.

9. For the commute data of Chapter 13 — $n = 36$, $\bar{x} = 28.4$, $s = 6$ — test
   $H_0: \mu = 30$ against a two-sided alternative at $\alpha = 0.05$.
   (a) Find $t$ and conclude.
   (b) The 95% confidence interval is $(26.3699,\ 30.4301)$. Confirm it agrees, and
   state the general rule.

10. Using the same commute data, test $H_0: \mu = 31$ instead.
    (a) Find $t$ and conclude.
    (b) Does this agree with the interval? Explain how one interval answers infinitely
    many tests at once.

11. Which of these is the correct definition of a p-value?
    (a) The probability that $H_0$ is true.
    (b) The probability that $H_0$ is false.
    (c) The probability of observing data at least as extreme as this, if $H_0$ is
    true.
    (d) The probability of making a Type I error.
    (e) The probability the result is due to chance.
    For each wrong one, say specifically what is wrong with it.

12. A test returns $p = 0.03$.
    (a) Conclude at $\alpha = 0.05$.
    (b) Conclude at $\alpha = 0.01$.
    (c) Explain why the same data give two different conclusions without either being
    a mistake.

13. In a quality-control setting, $H_0$ is "the batch meets specification".
    (a) Describe the Type I error in plain words and say who it costs.
    (b) Describe the Type II error and say who it costs.
    (c) Which would you rather make if the product is a brake component? A packet of
    crisps?

14. $H_0: \mu = 100$ is tested against $H_a: \mu > 100$ at $\alpha = 0.05$ with
    $n = 25$ and $\sigma = 15$.
    (a) Find the critical value of $\bar{X}$ — the value above which you reject.
    (b) Suppose the truth is $\mu = 106$. Find the power of the test.
    (c) Find $\beta$.

15. Repeat 14(b) with $n = 100$.
    (a) What is the power now?
    (b) State the general relationship between $n$ and power, and why it matters when
    a study reports "no significant effect".

16. Explain the trade-off between $\alpha$ and $\beta$. Why can you not simply set
    $\alpha = 0.001$ and be safe?

17. A sample of $n = 5{,}000$ gives $\bar{x} = 100.3$ where $H_0: \mu = 100$, with
    $\sigma = 5$.
    (a) Find $z$ and the two-tailed p-value.
    (b) Now find the same for $n = 25$ with the identical $\bar{x}$.
    (c) The effect is three-tenths of a unit in both cases. What should be reported
    alongside the p-value, and why?

18. A research group runs 20 independent tests at $\alpha = 0.05$, on data where every
    null hypothesis is in fact true.
    (a) How many "significant" results do they expect?
    (b) Find the probability they get at least one.
    (c) What does this imply about a paper that reports the one significant result out
    of twenty tests run?

19. Each of these sentences is wrong. Rewrite it.
    (a) "We accept the null hypothesis."
    (b) "$p = 0.20$, so there is no effect."
    (c) "$p = 0.001$, so the effect is large."
    (d) "The result is significant, so it is important."
    (e) "We reject $H_0$, so $H_a$ is true."

20. A test does not reject $H_0$. List three distinct reasons this could happen, only
    one of which is "$H_0$ is true".

21. A test statistic is $z = 1.85$.
    (a) Find the one-tailed p-value.
    (b) Find the two-tailed p-value.
    (c) At $\alpha = 0.05$, which tail choice changes the conclusion?

22. A student computes $t = -2.45$ with $\nu = 20$ for a left-tailed test, looks up
    $t_{0.05,20} = 1.7247$, and writes "$-2.45 < 1.7247$, so do not reject."
    Identify the error and give the correct conclusion.

23. List the assumptions behind the one-sample $t$ test, and for each say how you
    would check it and what you would do if it failed.

24. Write out the eight steps of a hypothesis test from memory, then use them to test
    the following: a college claims its graduates earn a mean starting salary of
    \$52,000. A sample of 36 graduates gives $\bar{x} = \$49{,}800$ with
    $s = \$7{,}200$. Test at $\alpha = 0.05$ against a two-sided alternative.

25. Two economists analyse the same dataset. One reports "$p = 0.049$, significant";
    the other reports "$p = 0.051$, not significant". Write a paragraph on what, if
    anything, separates these two findings.

### Answers to the extra problems

1. (a) $H_0: \mu = 500$, $H_a: \mu < 500$ — **left**. The inspector needs evidence of
   underfilling. (b) $H_0: \mu = \mu_0$, $H_a: \mu \neq \mu_0$ — **two-tailed**;
   "whether it has changed" names no direction. (c) $H_0: \mu = 3$,
   $H_a: \mu > 3$ — **right**; the buyer needs evidence the guarantee is breached.
   (d) $H_0: \mu = 18$, $H_a: \mu \neq 18$ — **two-tailed**. (e) $H_0: \mu = 7$,
   $H_a: \mu < 7$ — **left**; the regulator is checking the company's claim, and the
   claim is the thing requiring evidence. (f) $H_0: \mu = 250$,
   $H_a: \mu \neq 250$ — **two-tailed**; drift either way is a fault.
   (g) $H_0: \mu = \mu_0$, $H_a: \mu > \mu_0$ — **right**. (h) $H_0: \mu = 4$,
   $H_a: \mu < 4$ — **left**.

2. (a) The hypotheses are about $\bar{x}$, a **statistic**. Hypotheses are always
   about parameters; you know $\bar{x}$ exactly, so there is nothing to test.
   (b) $H_0$ has **no equals sign**, so there is no single value from which to compute
   a sampling distribution. Write $H_0: \mu = 100$ against $H_a: \mu < 100$.
   (c) The two hypotheses are **not exhaustive** — they say nothing about $\mu = 22$.
   $H_a$ must cover everything $H_0$ does not.
   (d) The alternative is being chosen **after seeing the data**, which makes the
   stated significance level a fiction. See question 4.

3. Because the whole calculation is conditional on $H_0$ being true, and you cannot
   compute a sampling distribution from a range of values. "If $\mu = 500$" pins down
   exactly one distribution for $\bar{X}$, from which a p-value follows; "if
   $\mu > 500$" pins down none, because the answer would differ for $\mu = 501$ and
   $\mu = 900$. $H_0$ has to be a specific claim precisely so that it can be
   contradicted by specific evidence.

4. (a) Whatever they say — typically 0.05.
   (b) Effectively **0.10**, because they would have run the test in the other
   direction had the data gone the other way, so there were two chances to reject at
   5% each.
   (c) Because it is invisible in the write-up. The reported test looks like a
   perfectly ordinary one-tailed test at 5%, and nothing in the published numbers
   reveals that the direction was chosen after the fact. The discipline of committing
   to $H_a$ before collecting data is the only thing that makes the stated error rate
   mean anything.

5. (a) $H_0: \mu = 500$ against $H_a: \mu \neq 500$.
   (b) SE $= 8/\sqrt{64} = 1.0000$, so $z = (497.5 - 500)/1.0 = -2.5000$. Note $z$,
   not $t$ — $\sigma$ is known.
   (c) $p = 2 \times \mathrm{P}(Z > 2.5) = 2(0.0062) = 0.0124$.
   (d) At $\alpha = 0.05$: $0.0124 < 0.05$, so **reject**. At $\alpha = 0.01$:
   $0.0124 > 0.01$, so **do not reject**. The data are the same; what changed is how
   much Type I error you were willing to tolerate. A result sitting between two
   conventional thresholds is exactly the situation in which reporting the p-value
   itself, rather than a verdict, earns its keep.

6. (a) $H_0: \mu = 100$ against $H_a: \mu > 100$ — right tail, because the programme
   claims to *raise* scores.
   (b) SE $= 12/\sqrt{16} = 3.0000$, so $t = (104-100)/3 = 1.3333$ with $\nu = 15$.
   (c) $t_{0.05,15} = 1.7531$. Since $1.3333 < 1.7531$, **do not reject**.
   (d) $p = 0.1012$ — about a one-in-ten chance of a sample mean this high or higher
   if the programme does nothing.

7. (a) $t_{0.025,15} = 2.1314$ and $p = 0.2024$, double the one-tailed value.
   (b) Because the two tests put the 5% allowance in different places. A one-tailed
   test concentrates all of it in the direction you predicted, making it easier to
   reject *in that direction* and impossible to reject in the other. Here the
   conclusion happens to be the same, but a statistic of, say, 1.80 would have been
   significant one-tailed and not two-tailed — which is why the tail must be chosen
   from the question, before the data, and never to secure a result.

8. (a) SE $= 4.9/\sqrt{49} = 0.7000$, so $t = (498.2 - 500)/0.7 = -2.5714$ with
   $\nu = 48$. The critical value is $t_{0.025,48} = 2.0106$, and
   $|-2.5714| > 2.0106$, so **reject $H_0$**.
   (b) $p = 0.0133$.
   (c) "The line is filling below 500 ml. A mean of 498.2 ml across 49 bottles is far
   enough below target that chance alone would produce it only about 1.3% of the time,
   so the machine should be recalibrated." Note that the actionable number is the
   1.8 ml shortfall, not the $t$ statistic.

9. (a) SE $= 6/\sqrt{36} = 1.0000$, so $t = (28.4 - 30)/1.0 = -1.6000$ with
   $\nu = 35$. The critical value is $t_{0.025,35} = 2.0301$, and
   $1.6 < 2.0301$, so **do not reject**. ($p = 0.1186$.)
   (b) 30 lies **inside** $(26.3699,\ 30.4301)$, so the interval does not reject it
   either. The general rule: a two-tailed test of $H_0: \mu = \mu_0$ at level
   $\alpha$ rejects precisely when $\mu_0$ falls outside the $100(1-\alpha)\%$
   confidence interval. They are the same statement rearranged.

10. (a) $t = (28.4 - 31)/1.0 = -2.6000$, and $2.6 > 2.0301$, so **reject**.
    (b) Yes — 31 is outside the interval. The interval is the complete set of null
    values that would *not* be rejected at the 5% level, so one interval answers the
    test for every possible $\mu_0$ at once. This is the strongest practical argument
    for reporting intervals rather than tests: a test answers one question, an interval
    answers all of them and shows you the effect size as well.

11. **(c) is correct.**
    (a) and (b) are wrong because the p-value is computed *assuming* $H_0$ is true —
    it is a probability about the data, not about the hypothesis. Classical statistics
    assigns no probability to a hypothesis at all.
    (d) is wrong because the Type I error rate is $\alpha$, which you choose in advance;
    the p-value is a property of the data you got.
    (e) is wrong in a subtler way: "due to chance" sounds like (a) in disguise. The
    p-value tells you how probable *this data* is under chance, not how probable chance
    is given this data — the same inversion as the prosecutor's fallacy in Chapter 6.

12. (a) $0.03 < 0.05$, so **reject**.
    (b) $0.03 > 0.01$, so **do not reject**.
    (c) Because $\alpha$ is not a property of the data — it is a decision about how
    much Type I error is acceptable, taken before the data arrive and on grounds
    outside the statistics. At the 1% level you have demanded stronger evidence, and
    this evidence does not meet that bar while meeting the looser one. Neither verdict
    is wrong; they are answers to different questions.

13. (a) **Type I**: rejecting a batch that was in fact fine. It costs the *producer* —
    good output is scrapped or reworked.
    (b) **Type II**: passing a batch that was defective. It costs the *customer*, and
    ultimately the producer's reputation.
    (c) For a **brake component**, a Type I error is vastly preferable: throw away good
    parts rather than ship bad ones, so set $\alpha$ high. For **crisps**, the
    asymmetry reverses — a slightly underweight packet is a minor matter against the
    cost of destroying good production, so a lower $\alpha$ is sensible. The choice of
    $\alpha$ is an economic decision, not a statistical one, and 0.05 is a convention
    rather than an answer.

14. (a) SE $= 15/\sqrt{25} = 3.0000$ and $z_{0.05} = 1.6449$, so you reject when
    $\bar{x} > 100 + 1.6449(3) = 104.9346$.
    (b) If $\mu = 106$, then
    $z = (104.9346 - 106)/3 = -0.3551$, so the power is
    $\mathrm{P}(Z > -0.3551) = 0.6387$.
    (c) $\beta = 1 - 0.6387 = 0.3613$ — a 36% chance of missing a real six-unit
    effect.

15. (a) SE $= 1.5000$, so the cut-off is $100 + 1.6449(1.5) = 102.4673$ and
    $z = (102.4673 - 106)/1.5 = -2.3551$, giving power $= 0.9907$.
    (b) Power rises with $n$, because the sampling distributions under $H_0$ and under
    the truth pull apart as the standard error shrinks. This is why "no significant
    effect" is uninterpretable without the sample size: at $n = 25$ this test misses a
    real effect more than a third of the time, so a non-rejection there is weak
    evidence of anything. The honest report of a null result always includes either the
    power or, better, the confidence interval showing which effect sizes remain
    consistent with the data.

16. For a fixed $n$, shrinking $\alpha$ moves the critical value further out, which
    makes rejection harder — and that reduces the chance of rejecting a true $H_0$
    (good) while also reducing the chance of rejecting a false one (bad). So $\beta$
    rises as $\alpha$ falls. You cannot simply set $\alpha = 0.001$ because you would
    then almost never reject anything, including every genuine effect in the data; a
    test with a Type I error rate of 0.001 and a Type II error rate of 0.8 is not a
    careful test, it is a broken one. The only way to reduce both at once is to
    increase $n$, which is why sample size is the real lever and $\alpha$ is only a
    dial for choosing which error you fear more.

17. (a) SE $= 5/\sqrt{5000} = 0.0707$, so $z = 0.3/0.0707 = 4.2426$ and
    $p < 0.0001$.
    (b) SE $= 5/\sqrt{25} = 1.0000$, so $z = 0.3000$ and $p = 0.7642$.
    (c) The **confidence interval**, or equivalently the effect size with its
    uncertainty. A difference of 0.3 units may be economically meaningless, and with
    5,000 observations the test detects it with overwhelming confidence — the p-value
    is answering "is the effect exactly zero?", which is almost never the interesting
    question. The interval answers "how big is it, and how sure are we?", which is.

18. (a) $20 \times 0.05 = 1$.
    (b) $1 - 0.95^{20} = 1 - 0.3585 = 0.6415$ — about a 64% chance.
    (c) That the single significant result is roughly what you would expect from
    nothing at all. A p-value of 0.04 means something when one test was run and very
    little when it is the best of twenty, because the 5% applies per test and not per
    paper. The remedies are to report every test run, to adjust the threshold, or to
    treat the finding as a hypothesis for a fresh dataset rather than as a result.

19. (a) "We **fail to reject** the null hypothesis." You never gather evidence *for*
    $H_0$; you only fail to find enough against it.
    (b) "$p = 0.20$, so there is **insufficient evidence of an effect**" — ideally with
    an interval showing what sizes remain plausible. Absence of evidence is not
    evidence of absence.
    (c) "$p = 0.001$, so the effect is **very unlikely to be zero**." A small p-value
    reflects the signal relative to the noise, and with a large $n$ a tiny effect
    produces a tiny p. Size is a separate question, answered by the estimate.
    (d) "The result is **statistically** significant." Importance is a judgement about
    magnitude and context, which no p-value contains.
    (e) "We reject $H_0$, so the data are **inconsistent with** $H_0$ at this level."
    Rejection is a decision under an error rate you have chosen to accept, not a proof;
    one test in twenty rejects a true null by design.

20. (i) **$H_0$ is true**, or close enough to true that the data cannot tell.
    (ii) **$n$ is too small** — the effect is real but the test lacks the power to
    detect it, as in question 15. (iii) **The data are too noisy**, with $s$ inflated
    by measurement error or an unmodelled source of variation, so a real signal is
    swamped. A fourth, worth noting: the test was the wrong one — a paired design
    analysed as independent, for example, which throws away the very structure that
    would have revealed the effect.

21. (a) $\mathrm{P}(Z > 1.85) = 0.0322$.
    (b) $2(0.0322) = 0.0643$.
    (c) The **one-tailed** test rejects at 5% ($0.0322 < 0.05$) while the two-tailed
    test does not ($0.0643 > 0.05$). This is exactly why the tail cannot be chosen
    after the fact — the same statistic yields opposite verdicts depending on a
    decision that must be made from the research question alone.

22. The student has compared a negative statistic with a positive critical value. For
    a **left-tailed** test the rejection region is $t < -t_{0.05,20} = -1.7247$, and
    $-2.45 < -1.7247$, so the correct conclusion is to **reject $H_0$**. The safe
    habit is to compare magnitudes — $|{-2.45}| = 2.45 > 1.7247$ — after confirming
    that the statistic falls on the side $H_a$ predicted. Here it does: a negative $t$
    is what a left-tailed alternative expects.

23. **Random sampling / independence.** Check it from the study design, not the data —
    no diagnostic reveals a clustered sample. If it fails, the standard error is wrong,
    usually too small, and the fix is a method that accounts for the structure.
    **Normality of the population, or a large enough $n$.** Check with a histogram or
    a normal plot of the sample. If it fails and $n$ is small, use a distribution-free
    test; if $n$ is large, the CLT covers you and nothing needs doing.
    **The data are quantitative and the parameter is a mean.** Check that the question
    is actually about a mean — for counts in categories you want Chapter 17 instead.
    **No outliers driving the result.** Check by inspecting the extremes and refitting
    without them. If a single observation reverses the verdict, say so in the write-up
    rather than choosing whichever version you prefer.

24. The steps: (1) state $H_0$ and $H_a$; (2) choose $\alpha$; (3) identify the test
    statistic and its distribution; (4) state the decision rule; (5) compute the
    standard error; (6) compute the statistic; (7) find the p-value or compare with the
    critical value; (8) state the conclusion in the language of the problem.
    Applying them: (1) $H_0: \mu = 52{,}000$ against $H_a: \mu \neq 52{,}000$.
    (2) $\alpha = 0.05$. (3) A one-sample $t$, since $\sigma$ is unknown, with
    $\nu = 35$. (4) Reject if $|t| > t_{0.025,35} = 2.0301$. (5) SE
    $= 7{,}200/\sqrt{36} = 1{,}200.0000$. (6)
    $t = (49{,}800 - 52{,}000)/1{,}200 = -1.8333$. (7) $|-1.8333| < 2.0301$, and
    $p = 0.0753$. (8) **Do not reject.** There is insufficient evidence at the 5% level
    that mean starting salary differs from \$52,000 — though note the 95% interval,
    $49{,}800 \pm 2.0301(1200) = (47{,}363.9,\ 52{,}236.1)$, is wide enough to include
    salaries well below the claim, so this is a statement about the data's
    inconclusiveness rather than a vindication of the college.

25. Essentially nothing separates them. The two p-values differ by 0.002, which is far
    less than the uncertainty in either, and the only reason they read as opposite
    findings is that 0.05 happens to sit between them. The threshold is a convention
    adopted for convenience, not a feature of the world, and nothing changes discretely
    at 0.05 — the evidence at 0.051 is almost exactly as strong as the evidence at
    0.049. The right response is to report both estimates with their confidence
    intervals, which will be very nearly identical, and to let the reader see that the
    two studies agree. Treating the pair as a disagreement is an artefact of
    dichotomising a continuous measure, and it is how a literature ends up with
    contradictory "findings" drawn from compatible data.

<!-- more-practice:end -->

---

# Chapter 15 — Comparing two means

> **The question:** two groups, two averages. Is the gap real?

## The same machinery, one parameter further

Chapter 14 tested a mean against a claimed number. This chapter tests a mean against
*another mean*, and almost nothing new is required — the test statistic is still

$$\frac{\text{what I got} - \text{what I expected}}{\text{how much it typically wobbles}}$$

What changes is that "what I got" is now a **difference**, $\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2$, and
the denominator has to describe how much a *difference* wobbles.

Under $H_0$ the two population means are equal, so the expected difference is zero.
That is why the numerator is usually just $\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2$ with nothing
subtracted.

## First, decide which design you have

This is the decision that matters, and getting it wrong invalidates everything after.

**Independent samples.** Two separate groups of people or items, with no pairing
between them. Thirty workers at one plant and thirty-five at another.

**Matched pairs.** *The same* subject measured twice, or subjects deliberately paired.
Before and after a training course; left hand and right hand; twins.

If each observation in group 1 has a natural partner in group 2, it is paired. If the
two groups could have different sizes without anything breaking, they are independent.

The two designs use different formulas, and the paired design is usually far more
powerful — because pairing removes the variation *between* subjects, leaving only the
variation in the thing you care about.

## Independent samples

The variance of a difference adds, from Chapter 7:

$$V(\bar{X}_1 - \bar{X}_2) = \frac{\sigma_1^2}{n_1} + \frac{\sigma_2^2}{n_2}$$

Note the **plus**. Subtracting the means does not subtract the uncertainties — two
noisy estimates make a noisier difference.

When the two population variances can be assumed equal, pool them:

$$s_p^2 = \frac{(n_1-1)s_1^2 + (n_2-1)s_2^2}{n_1 + n_2 - 2}$$

$$t = \frac{\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2}{s_p\sqrt{\dfrac{1}{n_1} + \dfrac{1}{n_2}}}
\qquad \nu = n_1 + n_2 - 2$$

The pooled variance is a weighted average of the two sample variances, weighted by
degrees of freedom. The larger sample has more say, which is as it should be.

### Worked example

Plant A: $n_1 = 30$ workers, mean output $\bar{x}_1 = 78$, $s_1 = 9$.
Plant B: $n_2 = 35$ workers, mean output $\bar{x}_2 = 72$, $s_2 = 11$.

**Is there a difference in mean output, at $\alpha = 0.05$?**

$H_0: \mu_1 = \mu_2$ against $H_a: \mu_1 \neq \mu_2$. Two-tailed — the question says
"a difference", with no direction.

$$s_p^2 = \frac{29(81) + 34(121)}{63} = \frac{2349 + 4114}{63} = 102.5873
\qquad s_p = 10.1285$$

$$\text{SE} = 10.1285\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{30} + \tfrac{1}{35}} = 2.5200$$

$$t = \frac{78 - 72}{2.5200} = 2.3809 \qquad \nu = 63$$

The critical value is $t_{0.025,63} = 1.9983$. Since $2.3809 > 1.9983$, **reject
$H_0$** — $p = 0.0203$.

**The interval tells you more.** A 95% confidence interval for $\mu_1 - \mu_2$:

$$6 \pm 1.9983(2.5200) = (0.9641,\ 11.0359)$$

It excludes zero, which is the same verdict. But it also says the gap could plausibly
be as small as **1 unit** or as large as **11** — so while the difference is real, its
size is poorly pinned down. A test alone would have hidden that.

## Matched pairs

Here the trick is to stop thinking about two samples at all. Compute the difference
for each pair, and you have **one** sample of differences. Then run the one-sample
test from Chapter 14 on it.

$$t = \frac{\bar{d}}{s_d/\sqrt{n}} \qquad \nu = n - 1$$

### Worked example

Eight trainees are tested before and after a course. The improvements are

$$4,\quad 6,\quad 2,\quad 8,\quad 5,\quad 3,\quad 7,\quad 5$$

$\bar{d} = 5.0$ and $s_d = 2.0$, so

$$\text{SE} = \frac{2}{\sqrt{8}} = 0.7071
\qquad t = \frac{5.0}{0.7071} = 7.0711 \qquad \nu = 7$$

That is enormous — $p = 0.0002$. **Reject $H_0$**: the course improves scores.

Notice how small the sample is. Eight pairs produced a far more decisive result than
65 workers did in the independent case, because pairing stripped out the differences
between trainees and left only the thing being measured.

## In Stata

*The snippets below assume the header and preamble from
[Chapter 1](01_what_statistics_is_for.md).*

```stata
use "DATA/cps_small.dta", clear

* independent samples, pooled
ttest wage, by(female)

* independent samples, not assuming equal variances
ttest wage, by(female) unequal

* from summary statistics, with no data
ttesti 30 78 9 35 72 11
```

For matched pairs the syntax differs — you give two variables rather than a grouping:

```stata
ttest before == after
```

Stata's output row labelled `diff` is a confidence interval for the difference. If it
excludes zero you reject, by exactly the equivalence from Chapter 14.

## In Python

```python
from scipy import stats

# independent, pooled
stats.ttest_ind(group1, group2)                      # equal_var=True by default

# independent, Welch (does not assume equal variances)
stats.ttest_ind(group1, group2, equal_var=False)

# matched pairs
stats.ttest_rel(before, after)

# from summary statistics
stats.ttest_ind_from_stats(78, 9, 30, 72, 11, 35)    # t = 2.3809
```

`ttest_rel` is the paired version, and using `ttest_ind` on paired data is the error
this chapter exists to prevent — it throws away the pairing and usually fails to find
a real effect.

## What goes wrong

**Treating paired data as independent.** The commonest and most expensive error here.
It discards the very structure that makes the design powerful.

**Subtracting the variances.** $V(X - Y) = V(X) + V(Y) - 2\text{COV}$, and for
independent samples the covariance is zero — so the variances **add**. Uncertainty
never cancels.

**Using the wrong degrees of freedom.** $n_1 + n_2 - 2$ for pooled independent samples;
$n - 1$ for paired, where $n$ is the number of *pairs*.

**Pooling when the variances are clearly unequal.** If one sample variance is several
times the other, use the unequal-variance version.

**Reporting only the test.** The interval for the difference tells you the plausible
*size* of the gap, which is usually what somebody actually wants.

## Exercises

1. Group 1: $n = 20$, $\bar{x} = 55$, $s = 8$. Group 2: $n = 25$, $\bar{x} = 49$,
   $s = 10$.
   (a) Compute $s_p^2$ and the standard error of the difference.
   (b) Compute $t$ and state the degrees of freedom.
   (c) Test at $\alpha = 0.05$, given $t_{0.025,43} = 2.0167$.

2. Six people are weighed before and after a diet. The losses in kg are
   $3, 5, 2, 4, 6, 4$.
   (a) Find $\bar{d}$ and $s_d$.
   (b) Test whether the diet produces a loss, at $\alpha = 0.05$, one-tailed, given
   $t_{0.05,5} = 2.0150$.

3. For each, say whether the design is independent or paired.
   (a) Test scores of 30 students taught by method A and 30 by method B.
   (b) Blood pressure of 25 patients before and after a drug.
   (c) Yields from 15 fields split in half, one half treated.
   (d) Salaries of 40 men and 45 women at a firm.

4. Explain why a paired design usually detects a smaller effect than an independent
   design with the same number of observations.

5. A 95% confidence interval for $\mu_1 - \mu_2$ is $(-2.4,\ 8.6)$. What do you
   conclude at $\alpha = 0.05$, and why?

### Answers

1. (a) $s_p^2 = [19(64) + 24(100)]/43 = (1216 + 2400)/43 = 84.093$, so
   $s_p = 9.1702$ and SE $= 9.1702\sqrt{1/20 + 1/25} = 9.1703(0.3) = 2.7511$.
   (b) $t = (55-49)/2.7511 = 2.1810$, $\nu = 43$.
   (c) $2.1810 > 2.0167$, so **reject** — the means differ.

2. (a) $\bar{d} = 24/6 = 4.0$; deviations $-1, 1, -2, 0, 2, 0$ give
   $\sum d^2 = 10$, so $s_d^2 = 2.0$ and $s_d = 1.4142$.
   (b) SE $= 1.4142/\sqrt{6} = 0.5774$, so $t = 4.0/0.5774 = 6.9282$ with $\nu = 5$.
   Since $6.9282 > 2.0150$, **reject** — the diet produces a loss.

3. (a) Independent. (b) **Paired** — same patients twice. (c) **Paired** — the two
   halves of one field are matched. (d) Independent.

4. Pairing removes the variation *between* subjects. In an independent design that
   person-to-person variation sits in the denominator as noise; in a paired design it
   cancels when you subtract, leaving only the variation in the effect itself. A
   smaller denominator means a larger $t$ for the same real effect.

5. **Do not reject.** The interval contains zero, so "no difference" remains plausible,
   and a two-tailed test at $\alpha = 0.05$ rejects exactly when the 95% interval
   excludes the claimed value — here, zero.

<!-- more-practice:start -->

## More practice

Twelve more. Question 4 is the one to get right first — every other question in this
chapter is downstream of deciding whether the design is paired or independent.

1. Two plants are compared. Plant A: $n_1 = 18$, $\bar{x}_1 = 64$, $s_1 = 9$.
   Plant B: $n_2 = 22$, $\bar{x}_2 = 58$, $s_2 = 11$. Assume equal variances.
   (a) Find $s_p^2$ and $s_p$.
   (b) Find the standard error of the difference.
   (c) Find $t$ and state $\nu$.
   (d) Test at $\alpha = 0.05$, two-tailed.
   (e) Find the p-value.

2. For the data in question 1, build a 95% confidence interval for
   $\mu_1 - \mu_2$.
   (a) Give the interval.
   (b) Confirm it agrees with your test, and say which of the two is more informative.

3. Seven trainees are tested before and after a course. The improvements are
   $2,\ 5,\ 3,\ 6,\ 4,\ 7,\ 5$.
   (a) Find $\bar{d}$ and $s_d$.
   (b) Find the standard error and $t$, with $\nu$.
   (c) Test at $\alpha = 0.05$ against a one-sided alternative that the course helps.
   (d) Find the p-value.

4. For each, state whether the design is independent or matched pairs.
   (a) Forty men and forty-five women at a firm, comparing salaries.
   (b) Twenty patients' blood pressure before and after a drug.
   (c) Fifteen fields split in half, one half treated with fertiliser.
   (d) Reaction times of each subject's left and right hand.
   (e) Exam marks of this year's class and last year's class.
   (f) Twenty-five pairs of twins, one of each pair in a programme.
   (g) The same thirty shops' sales in January and in July.
   (h) Thirty randomly assigned to treatment and thirty to control.

5. Compare question 1 (40 observations, not significant) with question 3 (7 pairs,
   significant). Explain how the smaller study produced the stronger result.

6. Why is the variance of $\bar{X}_1 - \bar{X}_2$ a **sum** of the two variances
   rather than a difference? Answer from the formula for the variance of a difference.

7. When would you use the unequal-variance (Welch) version rather than the pooled one?
   (a) Give the rule of thumb.
   (b) Say what goes wrong if you pool when you should not.
   (c) Why is Welch not simply the default?

8. State the degrees of freedom for (a) a pooled two-sample $t$ with $n_1 = 12$ and
   $n_2 = 15$, (b) a matched-pairs $t$ on 12 pairs, (c) a one-sample $t$ with
   $n = 27$. Note which two coincide and say why that is a coincidence.

9. A researcher has 20 subjects measured before and after a treatment, and runs an
   independent-samples test on the two columns of 20.
   (a) What has gone wrong?
   (b) Which direction does the error push the $t$ statistic, and why?
   (c) What would you expect to happen to the p-value?

10. A 95% interval for $\mu_1 - \mu_2$ is $(-1.8,\ 7.4)$.
    (a) What do you conclude at $\alpha = 0.05$?
    (b) A colleague writes "the two groups are the same". Correct them.

11. Using the data in question 1, test the one-sided alternative $\mu_1 > \mu_2$ at
    $\alpha = 0.05$.
    (a) What changes, and what is the conclusion?
    (b) Under what circumstances would this have been the legitimate test to run?

12. Write the sentence you would put in a report for question 3, in language a
    training manager would act on. It should contain the effect size, not just the
    verdict.

### Answers to the extra problems

1. (a) $s_p^2 = \dfrac{17(81) + 21(121)}{38} = \dfrac{1377 + 2541}{38} = 103.1053$,
   so $s_p = 10.1541$.
   (b) SE $= 10.1541\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{18} + \tfrac{1}{22}} =
   10.1541(0.3178) = 3.2272$.
   (c) $t = (64 - 58)/3.2272 = 1.8592$ with $\nu = 18 + 22 - 2 = 38$.
   (d) $t_{0.025,38} = 2.0244$. Since $1.8592 < 2.0244$, **do not reject** — there is
   insufficient evidence of a difference in mean output.
   (e) $p = 0.0707$.

2. (a) $6 \pm 2.0244(3.2272) = 6 \pm 6.5331 = (-0.5331,\ 12.5331)$.
   (b) The interval **contains zero**, so it does not reject $H_0: \mu_1 = \mu_2$ —
   the same verdict as the test, as it must be. The **interval is more informative**:
   it says the gap could plausibly be anywhere from Plant B being half a unit ahead to
   Plant A being twelve and a half ahead. "Not significant" sounds like "no
   difference"; the interval shows that a large difference is entirely consistent with
   this data, and that the study was simply too small to settle the matter.

3. (a) $\bar{d} = 32/7 = 4.5714$. The deviations square to
   $\sum d^2 - (\sum d)^2/n = 164 - 146.2857 = 17.7143$, so
   $s_d^2 = 17.7143/6 = 2.9524$ and $s_d = 1.7183$.
   (b) SE $= 1.7183/\sqrt{7} = 0.6494$, so $t = 4.5714/0.6494 = 7.0391$ with
   $\nu = 6$.
   (c) One-tailed, so the critical value is $t_{0.05,6} = 1.9432$. Since
   $7.0383 > 1.9432$, **reject** — the course improves scores.
   (d) $p = 0.0002$.

4. (a) Independent. (b) **Paired**. (c) **Paired** — the two halves of one field share
   its soil, weather and drainage. (d) **Paired**. (e) Independent. (f) **Paired** —
   twins are deliberately matched. (g) **Paired** — the same shops twice.
   (h) Independent. The test: if each observation in group 1 has a specific partner in
   group 2, it is paired; if the two groups could have had different sizes without
   anything breaking, they are independent.

5. Pairing removed the between-subject variation. In question 1 the denominator
   contains $s_p = 10.1541$, which is mostly differences *between workers* — a source
   of noise that has nothing to do with the question. In question 3 each trainee is
   compared with themselves, so that variation subtracts out and the denominator
   contains only $s_d = 1.7183$, the variation in the *improvement*. A standard
   deviation six times smaller more than compensates for having a fifth as many
   observations. The lesson is that design beats sample size: 7 well-paired
   observations here outperform 40 unpaired ones.

6. Because $V(X - Y) = V(X) + V(Y) - 2\text{COV}(X,Y)$, and with independent samples
   the covariance is zero, leaving $V(X) + V(Y)$. Uncertainty does not cancel when you
   subtract: two noisy estimates make a noisier difference, and the minus sign in the
   numerator has no effect on the denominator. (Note that a *paired* design is exactly
   the case where the covariance is **not** zero and is positive, so the $-2\text{COV}$
   term bites and the variance of the difference falls — which is question 5 written
   algebraically.)

7. (a) Pool only if the two sample variances are of similar size; the common rule of
   thumb is that the larger $s^2$ should be no more than about four times the smaller.
   Here $121/81 = 1.4938$, comfortably within that.
   (b) Pooling averages the two variances, so if they genuinely differ the pooled value
   overstates the uncertainty for the group with the smaller variance and understates
   it for the other. The resulting $t$ has neither the right value nor the right
   distribution, and the error is worst when the sample sizes are also unequal — the
   smaller sample's variance gets too little weight precisely when it matters most.
   (c) Welch is close to the default in modern practice, and Stata's `unequal` option
   is cheap to use. It is taught second because the pooled version makes the structure
   visible — you can see the weighted average of variances and the $n_1 + n_2 - 2$
   degrees of freedom — whereas Welch's fractional degrees of freedom are impossible to
   compute by hand and teach nothing.

8. (a) $12 + 15 - 2 = 25$. (b) $12 - 1 = 11$. (c) $27 - 1 = 26$. None of these
   coincide — but note how close (a) and (c) are, with 27 observations in each case.
   The difference is that the two-sample test estimated **two** means from the data and
   so pays twice; the one-sample test estimated one.

9. (a) The pairing has been discarded. The test now treats the 20 "before" and 20
   "after" values as two unrelated groups of people, when in fact each after-value
   belongs with a specific before-value.
   (b) **Downward**, usually sharply. The denominator becomes a pooled standard
   deviation reflecting differences between subjects, which is typically far larger
   than the standard deviation of the within-subject changes. The numerator is
   unchanged — $\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2$ equals $\bar{d}$ — so a bigger denominator means
   a smaller $t$.
   (c) It will be much larger, and a real effect may well come out "not significant".
   This is the error the chapter exists to prevent, and it is expensive because it
   produces a null result that looks perfectly respectable.

10. (a) The interval contains zero, so **do not reject** at the 5% level.
    (b) "The two groups are the same" overstates it. The interval says the difference
    could be anything from $-1.8$ to $7.4$, which includes values that would matter a
    great deal in practice. The correct statement is that this study cannot distinguish
    the two means — absence of evidence, not evidence of absence, and the width of the
    interval is the measure of how little was learned.

11. (a) Only the critical value: it becomes $t_{0.05,38} = 1.6860$, with all 5% in the
    upper tail. Since $1.8592 > 1.6860$, you would now **reject**, and the one-tailed
    p-value is 0.0354.
    (b) Only if the direction had been specified **before** seeing the data, on
    substantive grounds — for instance if Plant A had new equipment and the whole point
    of the study was to test whether it raised output. If the direction was chosen after
    noticing that $\bar{x}_1 > \bar{x}_2$, the stated 5% is really 10% and the
    rejection is not credible. Note how much hangs on this: the identical data give
    opposite verdicts, and nothing in the output distinguishes the honest version from
    the other one.

12. One acceptable answer: "Across seven trainees, scores rose by an average of 4.57
    points after the course, and the improvement was consistent — every trainee
    improved, by between 2 and 7 points. An improvement this consistent would arise by
    chance about twice in ten thousand such studies, so the course is doing something.
    The caution is the sample: seven trainees is enough to establish that the effect is
    real and not enough to pin down its size for the wider intake."

<!-- more-practice:end -->

# Chapter 16 — Comparing several means: ANOVA

> **The question:** three groups, three averages. Why can you not just run three
> $t$ tests?

## Why not just do it three times

With three groups there are three pairwise comparisons: 1 vs 2, 1 vs 3, 2 vs 3. With
four groups there are six. The temptation is obvious and the problem is the one from
Chapter 14.

Each test at $\alpha = 0.05$ has a 5% chance of a false positive when nothing is
going on. Run three independent tests on groups that are genuinely identical and the
chance that **at least one** comes out significant is

$$1 - (0.95)^3 = 0.1426$$

Not 5% but **14%**. With six comparisons it is 26%. You have quietly abandoned the
error rate you claimed to be using — and this is just the twenty-tests simulation
from Chapter 14, in a form that looks respectable.

**Analysis of variance** asks one question instead, at one $\alpha$:

$$H_0: \mu_1 = \mu_2 = \mu_3 \qquad\text{against}\qquad
H_a: \text{at least one mean differs}$$

Note what $H_a$ does *not* say. It does not name which. A rejection tells you the
means are not all equal and nothing more.

## The idea: two kinds of variation

Here is the move that gives the method its name. Take all the observations together
and ask where their variation comes from. There are exactly two sources.

**Between groups.** The group means differ from each other. If the treatments really
do something, this is large.

**Within groups.** Observations differ from their own group's mean. This is noise —
it would be there even if every treatment were identical.

The test compares them:

$$F = \frac{\text{variation between groups}}{\text{variation within groups}}
= \frac{\text{signal}}{\text{noise}}$$

Which is the same signal-to-noise ratio as every test statistic in this course. If
$H_0$ is true, the two estimate the same thing and $F$ sits near 1. If the group
means genuinely differ, the numerator inflates and $F$ grows.

## The arithmetic

With $k$ groups and $N$ observations in total:

$$\text{SSB} = \sum_j n_j(\bar{x}_j - \bar{x})^2
\qquad
\text{SSW} = \sum_j \sum_i (x_{ij} - \bar{x}_j)^2$$

where $\bar{x}$ is the **grand mean** over everything. These add to the total:
$\text{SST} = \text{SSB} + \text{SSW}$, which is a useful arithmetic check.

Divide each by its degrees of freedom to get a **mean square**:

$$\text{MSB} = \frac{\text{SSB}}{k-1}
\qquad
\text{MSW} = \frac{\text{SSW}}{N-k}
\qquad
F = \frac{\text{MSB}}{\text{MSW}}$$

with $\nu_1 = k-1$ and $\nu_2 = N-k$. **Numerator first**, as Chapter 10 insisted.

The test is always **one-tailed, upper**. Only a large $F$ is evidence against $H_0$;
a small $F$ means the groups look alike, which is what $H_0$ claims.

## Worked example

Three production lines, five days each:

| Line A | 12 | 15 | 14 | 16 | 13 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Line B** | 18 | 20 | 17 | 19 | 21 |
| **Line C** | 14 | 13 | 16 | 15 | 12 |

Group means are **14, 19, 14**, and the grand mean is $235/15 = 15.67$.

**Between:**

$$\text{SSB} = 5(14-15.67)^2 + 5(19-15.67)^2 + 5(14-15.67)^2 = 83.3333$$

**Within:** each group's own squared deviations — 10, 10 and 10 — so
$\text{SSW} = 30$.

Check: $83.3333 + 30 = 113.3333$, which is SST computed directly.

$$\text{MSB} = \frac{83.3333}{2} = 41.6667
\qquad
\text{MSW} = \frac{30}{12} = 2.5000$$

$$F = \frac{41.6667}{2.5000} = 16.6667 \qquad \nu_1 = 2,\ \nu_2 = 12$$

The critical value is $F_{0.05,2,12} = 3.8853$. Since $16.6667 > 3.8853$, **reject
$H_0$** — $p = 0.0003$. The three lines do not all have the same mean output.

**And then stop.** The $F$ test says "not all equal". It does not say Line B is the
odd one out — even though the means make that obvious here, the test itself has not
established it. Establishing *which* needs a follow-up procedure designed to control
the error rate across multiple comparisons, which is beyond this course.

## The ANOVA table

Results are conventionally laid out like this, and you should be able to read one or
fill in its gaps:

| Source | SS | df | MS | $F$ |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Between groups | 83.3333 | 2 | 41.6667 | 16.6667 |
| Within groups | 30.0000 | 12 | 2.5000 | |
| **Total** | **113.3333** | **14** | | |

Every number in it follows from the ones around it: the df column sums, the SS column
sums, each MS is its SS over its df, and $F$ is the ratio of the two MS values. A
common exam question gives you three entries and asks for the rest.

## The assumptions

ANOVA assumes the groups are **independent**, each population is roughly **normal**,
and the population **variances are equal**. The third is the one people forget, and
it is the reason MSW can be pooled across groups at all. As a rough field check, the
largest group standard deviation should be under about twice the smallest.

## In Stata

*The snippets below assume the header and preamble from
[Chapter 1](01_what_statistics_is_for.md).*

```stata
use "DATA/cps_small.dta", clear

oneway wage educ, tabulate
```

`oneway` prints the full ANOVA table — between, within, total, with the $F$ and its
$p$-value. The `tabulate` option adds each group's mean and standard deviation, which
is how you check the equal-variance assumption.

## In Python

```python
from scipy import stats

A = [12, 15, 14, 16, 13]
B = [18, 20, 17, 19, 21]
C = [14, 13, 16, 15, 12]

F, p = stats.f_oneway(A, B, C)
print(F, p)              # 16.6667  0.000344
```

To see the pieces rather than just the answer:

```python
import numpy as np
groups = [A, B, C]
allv = np.concatenate(groups)
gm = allv.mean()

SSB = sum(len(g)*(np.mean(g) - gm)**2 for g in groups)
SSW = sum(((np.array(g) - np.mean(g))**2).sum() for g in groups)

print(SSB, SSW, SSB + SSW)                    # 83.33  30.0  113.33
print((SSB/2) / (SSW/12))                     # 16.6667
```

## What goes wrong

**Running pairwise $t$ tests instead.** Three tests at 5% give a 14% false-positive
rate. That is the whole reason this method exists.

**Concluding which group differs.** The $F$ test does not say. It says "not all
equal".

**Reversing the degrees of freedom.** $\nu_1 = k-1$ on top, $\nu_2 = N-k$ below, and
$F_{2,12} \neq F_{12,2}$.

**Using a two-tailed critical value.** ANOVA is always upper-tailed.

**Ignoring unequal variances.** If one group is far more variable than the others,
pooling is not legitimate.

**Forgetting that SSB + SSW = SST.** It is a free check, and it catches most
arithmetic slips.

## Exercises

1. Four groups, 8 observations each. State $\nu_1$, $\nu_2$ and the total degrees of
   freedom.

2. Complete this ANOVA table.

   | Source | SS | df | MS | $F$ |
   |---|---|---|---|---|
   | Between | 48 | 3 | ? | ? |
   | Within | ? | 20 | 4 | |
   | Total | ? | ? | | |

3. Three teaching methods give means 70, 74 and 78 with 10 students each. SSW is 900.
   (a) Find the grand mean and SSB.
   (b) Find $F$.
   (c) Test at $\alpha = 0.05$, given $F_{0.05,2,27} = 3.354$.

4. Explain, in your own words, why running six pairwise tests at $\alpha = 0.05$ is
   not a 5% procedure.

5. An ANOVA gives $F = 0.42$. What do you conclude, and could a small $F$ ever be
   evidence against $H_0$?

### Answers

1. $k = 4$ and $N = 32$, so $\nu_1 = 3$, $\nu_2 = 28$, and the total is 31.

2. MSB $= 48/3 = 16$. SSW $= 4 \times 20 = 80$. $F = 16/4 = 4$. SST $= 48 + 80 = 128$
   on $3 + 20 = 23$ degrees of freedom.

3. (a) Grand mean $= (70+74+78)/3 = 74$. SSB $= 10(16) + 10(0) + 10(16) = 320$.
   (b) MSB $= 320/2 = 160$; MSW $= 900/27 = 33.33$; $F = 160/33.33 = 4.80$.
   (c) $4.80 > 3.354$, so **reject** — the three methods do not all have the same mean.

4. Each test carries its own 5% chance of a false positive. Across six tests those
   chances accumulate, so the probability that *at least one* is significant when all
   groups are identical rises to about 26%. The claimed 5% applies to a single
   comparison, not to the whole family of them.

5. **Do not reject.** An $F$ below 1 means the between-group variation is smaller than
   the within-group noise, which is exactly what $H_0$ predicts. A small $F$ is never
   evidence *against* $H_0$ — which is why the test is one-tailed.

<!-- more-practice:start -->

## More practice

Eight more. The arithmetic is mechanical; what is tested is whether you can say what
the $F$ statistic is a ratio *of*, and what rejecting it does and does not tell you.

1. Three teaching methods are compared with six students each. The group means are
   22, 26 and 30, and SSW $= 240$.
   (a) Find the grand mean and SSB.
   (b) State $\nu_1$, $\nu_2$ and SST.
   (c) Find MSB, MSW and $F$.
   (d) Test at $\alpha = 0.05$, given $F_{0.05,2,15} = 3.6823$.
   (e) Find the p-value.

2. Explain why running three separate $t$ tests on the three pairs would be wrong.
   (a) How many pairwise tests are there with three groups? With five?
   (b) With three groups and $\alpha = 0.05$ each, find the probability of at least one
   false positive, assuming independence.
   (c) Repeat for five groups.

3. Complete this ANOVA table.

   | Source | SS | df | MS | $F$ |
   |---|---|---|---|---|
   | Between | 120 | 3 | ? | ? |
   | Within | 280 | 36 | ? | |
   | Total | ? | ? | | |

4. In question 3, how many groups were there and how many observations in total?

5. An $F$ statistic comes out as 0.84.
   (a) Can you reject $H_0$? Answer without a table.
   (b) What does an $F$ below 1 tell you about the two sources of variation?

6. State the three assumptions behind one-way ANOVA, and for each say how you would
   check it.

7. A researcher rejects $H_0$ in a four-group ANOVA and concludes "all four means
   differ from each other."
   (a) What is wrong?
   (b) What does rejection actually establish?
   (c) What would you do next?

8. Explain why the $F$ test is always **upper-tailed**, when the $t$ test for two
   means is usually two-tailed. Your answer should mention what large and small values
   of $F$ each imply.

### Answers to the extra problems

1. (a) Grand mean $= (22 + 26 + 30)/3 = 26.0000$, since the groups are equal in size.
   SSB $= 6(22-26)^2 + 6(26-26)^2 + 6(30-26)^2 = 96 + 0 + 96 = 192$.
   (b) $k = 3$ so $\nu_1 = 2$; $N = 18$ so $\nu_2 = 15$. SST $= 192 + 240 = 432$.
   (c) MSB $= 192/2 = 96.0000$; MSW $= 240/15 = 16.0000$; $F = 96/16 = 6.0000$.
   (d) $6.0000 > 3.6823$, so **reject $H_0$** — the three method means are not all
   equal.
   (e) $p = 0.0122$.

2. Because the error rate compounds. Each test carries its own 5% chance of a false
   positive, and the chance that *at least one* of several tests misfires is much
   larger than 5%. ANOVA asks the question once, at 5%, which is why it exists.
   (a) $\binom{3}{2} = 3$ with three groups; $\binom{5}{2} = 10$ with five.
   (b) $1 - 0.95^3 = 1 - 0.8574 = 0.1426$.
   (c) $1 - 0.95^{10} = 1 - 0.5987 = 0.4013$ — a better than two-in-five chance of
   declaring a difference that is not there, from a procedure each of whose steps was
   run "at 5%".

3. MSB $= 120/3 = 40.0000$. MSW $= 280/36 = 7.7778$.
   $F = 40/7.7778 = 5.1429$. SST $= 120 + 280 = 400$ with
   $3 + 36 = 39$ degrees of freedom. The row of totals is a free arithmetic check: if
   SSB and SSW do not add to SST, something has gone wrong upstream.

4. $\nu_1 = k - 1 = 3$, so $k = 4$ groups. $\nu_2 = N - k = 36$, so
   $N = 36 + 4 = 40$ observations — and the total degrees of freedom, 39, is
   $N - 1$ as it should be.

5. (a) **No**, and you do not need a table. $F$ is a ratio of two estimates of the same
   variance, so under $H_0$ it should hover around 1; every critical value for $F$ is
   greater than 1, so any $F$ below 1 is automatically in the non-rejection region.
   (b) That the between-group variation is *smaller* than the within-group variation —
   the group means are closer together than random assignment alone would typically
   produce. It is not evidence of anything beyond "no detectable difference", though a
   persistently tiny $F$ across studies can hint that the grouping is capturing
   something that was controlled rather than randomised.

6. **Independence** of observations, within and between groups: check from the design,
   not the data. **Normality** within each group: check with histograms or normal plots
   per group, though with reasonable group sizes the CLT makes ANOVA fairly tolerant.
   **Equal variances** across groups: compare the group standard deviations, and as a
   rough field check the largest should be no more than about twice the smallest. This
   last one matters most, because MSW pools the groups' variances and pooling is only
   meaningful if there is a common variance to pool.

7. (a) ANOVA tests a single composite null, $H_0: \mu_1 = \mu_2 = \mu_3 = \mu_4$.
   Rejecting it says that claim is false — which it would be if just one mean differed
   and the other three were identical.
   (b) Only that **not all** the means are equal. It identifies no particular pair and
   gives no ranking.
   (c) Follow up with post-hoc pairwise comparisons that adjust for multiple testing,
   or — better in most applied work — report the group means with their confidence
   intervals and let the reader see which gaps are large and which are not.

8. Because $F$ is a ratio of between-group to within-group variance, and only one
   direction is evidence against $H_0$. A **large** $F$ means the group means are
   spread out by more than within-group noise can account for, which is exactly what
   "the means differ" looks like. A **small** $F$ means the group means are unusually
   close together, which is not evidence that they differ — it is, if anything, the
   opposite. There is nothing in the lower tail worth rejecting for, so the whole
   $\alpha$ goes in the upper one. The two-tailed $t$ is different because a difference
   of means can be negative, and a negative difference is still a difference; squaring
   things into a variance ratio throws that sign away.

<!-- more-practice:end -->

---

# Chapter 17 — Categorical data: proportions and chi-squared

> **The question:** everything so far has tested a mean. What if the data is
> categories rather than numbers?

## Counting instead of measuring

Up to now the data has been quantitative — wages, times, weights — and the parameter
of interest has been $\mu$. This chapter handles data that comes as **counts in
categories**: how many customers were satisfied, how many items came from each
supplier, whether approval depends on borrower type.

Two tools, and the second does most of the work.

## A proportion is a mean in disguise

Code each observation as 1 for a success and 0 otherwise. Then the sample proportion

$$\hat{p} = \frac{\text{number of successes}}{n}$$

is just the **mean of those 0s and 1s**. Which means everything from Chapters 11 to 14
carries over unchanged: $\hat{p}$ is a statistic, it has a sampling distribution, and
the CLT makes that distribution approximately normal.

Its standard error is

$$\text{SE}(\hat{p}) = \sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)}{n}}$$

and the confidence interval has the usual shape:

$$\hat{p} \pm z_{\alpha/2}\sqrt{\frac{\hat{p}(1-\hat{p})}{n}}$$

### The $z$, not $t$, surprise

Look carefully: the standard error was **estimated from the data** — we substituted
$\hat{p}$ for the unknown $p$ — and yet the critical value is $z$.

This is the counterexample promised in Chapter 10. "Estimated denominator means $t$"
is *not* the rule. The $t$ distribution arises specifically because $s$ and $\bar{x}$
are independent and $s^2$ has a chi-squared distribution — a situation that does not
arise here. For proportions, the normal approximation is used directly.

The approximation needs $n\hat{p} \geq 5$ and $n(1-\hat{p}) \geq 5$, the same
condition as the binomial in Chapter 8, for the same reason.

### Worked example

In a sample of 400 customers, 168 report being satisfied, so $\hat{p} = 0.42$.

$$\text{SE} = \sqrt{\frac{0.42(0.58)}{400}} = 0.0247$$

$$0.42 \pm 1.96(0.0247) = (0.3716,\ 0.4684)$$

Check the condition: $400(0.42) = 168$ and $400(0.58) = 232$, both comfortably above
5. The interval is valid, and we are 95% confident the population satisfaction rate
lies between about **37% and 47%**.

## Chi-squared: comparing counts with expectations

The second tool handles tables of counts, and it is the same statistic in both of its
uses:

$$\chi^2 = \sum \frac{(O - E)^2}{E}$$

where $O$ is what you **observed** and $E$ is what you would **expect** if $H_0$ were
true.

Read the formula. Each term is a squared discrepancy, scaled by how big a discrepancy
you should expect in that cell. Scaling matters: being 10 off when you expected 20 is
serious, while being 10 off when you expected 1,000 is nothing.

Chapter 10 said chi-squared is built for exactly this — add up squared errors — and
this is the payoff. The test is always **upper-tailed**: only large discrepancies are
evidence against $H_0$.

## Use one: goodness of fit

Does one categorical variable follow a claimed distribution?

A shop expects its three branches to take 30%, 40% and 30% of orders. Out of 100
orders it observes 28, 42 and 30.

Expected counts: $100(0.30) = 30$, $100(0.40) = 40$, $100(0.30) = 30$.

$$\chi^2 = \frac{(28-30)^2}{30} + \frac{(42-40)^2}{40} + \frac{(30-30)^2}{30}
= 0.1333 + 0.1000 + 0 = 0.2333$$

Degrees of freedom are $k - 1 = 2$. The critical value is $\chi^2_{0.05,2} = 5.9915$.

Since $0.2333 < 5.9915$, **do not reject** — $p = 0.8899$. The observed split is
entirely consistent with the claimed one.

## Use two: independence

Does one categorical variable depend on another? This is Chapter 5's independence
question, now with a formal test behind it.

A firm classifies 200 applications by outcome and by whether the applicant was
referred:

| | Referred | Not referred | Total |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Accepted** | 60 | 40 | 100 |
| **Rejected** | 30 | 70 | 100 |
| **Total** | 90 | 110 | 200 |

Under independence, Chapter 5 says $\mathrm{P}(A \cap B) = \mathrm{P}(A)\mathrm{P}(B)$.
Turning that into an expected count gives the rule you actually use:

$$E = \frac{\text{row total} \times \text{column total}}{\text{grand total}}$$

So the top-left cell expects $100 \times 90 / 200 = 45$. All four expected counts are
45, 55, 45, 55.

$$\chi^2 = \frac{(60-45)^2}{45} + \frac{(40-55)^2}{55} + \frac{(30-45)^2}{45}
+ \frac{(70-55)^2}{55} = 18.1818$$

Degrees of freedom for a table are $(r-1)(c-1) = 1 \times 1 = 1$. The critical value
is $\chi^2_{0.05,1} = 3.8415$.

Since $18.1818 > 3.8415$, **reject** — $p = 0.00002$. Acceptance and referral are not
independent: referred applicants are accepted far more often.

### The degrees of freedom

| Test | df |
|---|---|
| Goodness of fit, $k$ categories | $k - 1$ |
| Independence, $r \times c$ table | $(r-1)(c-1)$ |

Both follow the same logic as Chapter 3's: once the margins are fixed, only that many
cells are free to vary.

**The condition:** every expected count should be at least 5. Note *expected*, not
observed — a cell can observe zero and still be fine if its expectation is large
enough.

## In Stata

*The snippets below assume the header and preamble from
[Chapter 1](01_what_statistics_is_for.md).*

```stata
use "DATA/cps_small.dta", clear

* confidence interval for a proportion
cii proportions 400 168, wald

* a test of independence
tabulate female black, chi2 expected
```

Use the `wald` option on `cii`: without it Stata reports an exact binomial interval,
which is a better interval but is **not** the formula above, so your hand calculation
will not match.

The `expected` option on `tabulate` prints the expected counts alongside the observed
ones, which is how you check the all-expectations-above-5 condition.

## In Python

```python
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats

# goodness of fit
stats.chisquare([28, 42, 30], f_exp=[30, 40, 30])     # 0.2333, p = 0.8899

# independence
table = np.array([[60, 40], [30, 70]])
chi2, p, dof, expected = stats.chi2_contingency(table, correction=False)
print(chi2, dof, p)      # 18.1818  1  0.00002
print(expected)          # [[45. 55.] [45. 55.]]
```

`chi2_contingency` returns the expected counts as its fourth value, so you can check
the condition without computing them by hand. Set `correction=False` to match the
formula above — with `True` it applies a continuity correction for $2\times2$ tables
and your arithmetic will differ.

## What goes wrong

**Using $t$ for a proportion.** It is $z$, despite the estimated standard error. This
is the exception that disproves the rule people invent.

**Checking observed counts instead of expected.** The condition is on $E$.

**Wrong degrees of freedom.** $k-1$ for goodness of fit; $(r-1)(c-1)$ for a table. For
a $2\times2$ table that is 1, not 3.

**Using percentages in the formula.** $\chi^2$ needs **counts**. Feeding it
percentages changes the answer entirely.

**A two-tailed critical value.** The chi-squared test is always upper-tailed.

**Concluding causation from independence.** Rejecting independence says the two
variables are associated, not that one causes the other — Chapter 4's warning, in a
new costume.

## Exercises

1. In a sample of 250, 95 prefer brand A.
   (a) Find $\hat{p}$ and its standard error.
   (b) Build a 95% confidence interval.
   (c) Check the condition for the approximation.

2. A die is rolled 120 times with these counts: 18, 22, 19, 21, 20, 20.
   (a) What are the expected counts if the die is fair?
   (b) Compute $\chi^2$ and state the degrees of freedom.
   (c) Test at $\alpha = 0.05$, given $\chi^2_{0.05,5} = 11.0705$.

3. A $3 \times 4$ contingency table is tested for independence. How many degrees of
   freedom?

4. In a $2\times2$ table the row totals are 80 and 120, the column totals 100 and
   100, and the grand total 200. Find all four expected counts.

5. A researcher tests independence of two variables, rejects $H_0$, and writes "the
   first variable causes the second". What is wrong?

### Answers

1. (a) $\hat{p} = 95/250 = 0.38$;
   SE $= \sqrt{0.38(0.62)/250} = \sqrt{0.000942} = 0.0307$.
   (b) $0.38 \pm 1.96(0.0307) = (0.3198,\ 0.4402)$.
   (c) $250(0.38) = 95$ and $250(0.62) = 155$, both well above 5. Valid.

2. (a) $120/6 = 20$ for each face.
   (b) Squared deviations are $4, 4, 1, 1, 0, 0$, each divided by 20, giving
   $\chi^2 = 10/20 = 0.50$ with $\nu = 5$.
   (c) $0.50 < 11.0705$, so **do not reject** — no evidence the die is unfair.

3. $(3-1)(4-1) = 6$.

4. Row 1: $80(100)/200 = 40$ and $40$. Row 2: $120(100)/200 = 60$ and $60$. All four
   are 40, 40, 60, 60, and they reproduce the margins — a useful check.

5. The test establishes **association**, not causation. Either variable could drive
   the other, or a third could drive both. The chi-squared test says the two are not
   independent and is silent on why.

<!-- more-practice:start -->

## More practice

Eight more. Watch the degrees of freedom in questions 2 and 3 — they are the single
commonest slip in this chapter, and a correct $\chi^2$ compared against the wrong row
of the table scores nothing.

1. In a survey of 300 customers, 108 say they would recommend the product.
   (a) Find $\hat{p}$ and its standard error.
   (b) Build a 95% confidence interval.
   (c) Check the condition for the approximation.
   (d) Why is the critical value $z$ and not $t$, when the standard error was
   estimated from the data?

2. A firm expects its four regional offices to handle equal shares of enquiries. Out
   of 200 enquiries the counts are 42, 55, 61 and 42.
   (a) State the hypotheses and the expected counts.
   (b) Compute $\chi^2$ and state the degrees of freedom.
   (c) Test at $\alpha = 0.05$, given $\chi^2_{0.05,3} = 7.8147$.
   (d) Find the p-value.

3. Two hundred and fifty applicants are classified by outcome and by which of three
   channels they applied through:

   | | A | B | C | Total |
   |---|---|---|---|---|
   | **Accepted** | 30 | 45 | 25 | 100 |
   | **Rejected** | 50 | 35 | 65 | 150 |
   | **Total** | 80 | 80 | 90 | 250 |

   (a) Compute all six expected counts.
   (b) Compute $\chi^2$ and state the degrees of freedom.
   (c) Test at $\alpha = 0.05$, given $\chi^2_{0.05,2} = 5.9915$.
   (d) State the conclusion in words.

4. State the degrees of freedom for a test of independence on a table that is
   (a) $2 \times 2$, (b) $4 \times 5$, (c) $2 \times 6$, (d) $3 \times 3$. Then say why
   a $2 \times 2$ table has 1 and not 3.

5. A researcher's $2 \times 2$ table has one cell with an observed count of 2 and an
   expected count of 8.
   (a) Is the condition satisfied?
   (b) Which of the two numbers does the condition apply to, and why does that make
   sense?

6. A student converts a table of counts into row percentages, then feeds the
   percentages into $\chi^2 = \sum (O-E)^2/E$.
   (a) What goes wrong?
   (b) Why does the statistic depend on the counts rather than only the proportions?

7. Explain why the chi-squared test is always upper-tailed, and what an unusually
   *small* $\chi^2$ would suggest.

8. A test of independence between an applicant's channel and their outcome rejects
   $H_0$. A manager concludes "channel C is causing rejections."
   (a) What does the test establish?
   (b) Give two explanations consistent with the result that are not causal.

### Answers to the extra problems

1. (a) $\hat{p} = 108/300 = 0.3600$ and
   SE $= \sqrt{0.36(0.64)/300} = \sqrt{0.000768} = 0.0277$.
   (b) $0.36 \pm 1.96(0.0277) = 0.36 \pm 0.0543 = (0.3057,\ 0.4143)$.
   (c) $n\hat{p} = 108$ and $n(1-\hat{p}) = 192$, both far above 5. Valid.
   (d) Because the $t$ distribution arises from a very specific construction — a
   standard normal over the square root of an independent scaled chi-squared, which is
   what you get when $s$ and $\bar{x}$ are computed from normal data. For a proportion
   there is no separate $s$: the standard error is a function of $\hat{p}$ itself, so
   the ratio is not a $t$ variable. "Estimated denominator means $t$" is a useful
   habit and not a rule, and this is the counterexample.

2. (a) $H_0$: the four offices handle equal shares, so each expected count is
   $200/4 = 50$. $H_a$: the shares are not all equal.
   (b) $\chi^2 = \dfrac{64 + 25 + 121 + 64}{50} = \dfrac{274}{50} = 5.4800$ with
   $\nu = k - 1 = 3$.
   (c) $5.4800 < 7.8147$, so **do not reject** — the observed split is consistent with
   equal shares.
   (d) $p = 0.1398$.

3. (a) Each is row total times column total over 250:
   Accepted — $32.0000$, $32.0000$, $36.0000$; Rejected — $48.0000$, $48.0000$,
   $54.0000$. They reproduce the margins, which is the check.
   (b) $\chi^2 = \dfrac{4}{32} + \dfrac{169}{32} + \dfrac{121}{36} + \dfrac{4}{48}
   + \dfrac{169}{48} + \dfrac{121}{54} = 14.6128$ with
   $\nu = (2-1)(3-1) = 2$.
   (c) $14.6128 > 5.9915$, so **reject $H_0$** ($p = 0.0007$).
   (d) Outcome and channel are not independent. Channel B's applicants are accepted far
   more often than its share of applications would suggest — 45 accepted against an
   expected 32 — while channel C's are accepted far less, 25 against an expected 36.

4. (a) 1. (b) 12. (c) 5. (d) 4. A $2 \times 2$ table has 1 rather than 3 because the
   row and column totals are fixed by the data itself, not supplied from outside: once
   you know the margins and any **one** cell, the other three are forced by
   subtraction. Only one number in the table is free to vary, so there is one degree of
   freedom. Counting four cells and subtracting one is the wrong arithmetic and happens
   to give 3.

5. (a) **Yes.** The condition is that every *expected* count be at least 5, and 8
   clears it.
   (b) It applies to the **expected** count. That makes sense because $E$ is the
   denominator in each term of $\chi^2 = \sum (O-E)^2/E$ — a tiny expectation makes the
   term explode and destroys the chi-squared approximation, regardless of what was
   observed. A cell can observe zero and be perfectly well behaved provided enough was
   expected there.

6. (a) The test statistic changes, usually becoming far too small. Converting 60 out of
   100 into "60%" and 600 out of 1,000 into "60%" makes two completely different bodies
   of evidence look identical, and $\chi^2$ computed on percentages effectively asserts
   $n = 100$ for every row.
   (b) Because the strength of evidence depends on how much data there is, not only on
   the pattern. A 10-point gap in proportions from 40 observations is noise; the same
   gap from 4,000 is decisive. Proportions describe the pattern, counts carry the
   sample size, and $\chi^2$ needs both — which is exactly why it is built from counts.

7. The statistic is a sum of squared discrepancies, so it is zero when observation
   matches expectation exactly and grows as they diverge — in either direction, since
   the squaring removes the sign. Only *large* values are evidence against $H_0$, so
   all of $\alpha$ goes in the upper tail. An unusually **small** $\chi^2$ means the
   data fit the expectation more closely than chance would normally allow, which is not
   evidence for $H_0$ so much as grounds for suspecting the data were not generated the
   way they were described — the classic reason to be sceptical of results that are
   *too* good.

8. (a) Only **association**: the distribution of outcomes differs across channels by
   more than chance would explain. It identifies no cause and no direction.
   (b) (i) **Selection** — stronger applicants may choose channel B, so the channel is
   marking a difference in applicants rather than creating one. (ii) **A confounder** —
   channels may differ in what roles they advertise, in which case both channel and
   outcome are driven by the type of position. A third: the channels may be used at
   different times of year, with different acceptance rates for unrelated reasons.

<!-- more-practice:end -->

---

# Chapter 18 — Regression

> **The question:** correlation said the two variables move together. Can you put a
> line through them and predict?

## From describing to predicting

Chapter 4 measured whether $x$ and $y$ move together and how tightly. It stopped
there, deliberately — $r$ is symmetric, and it tells you nothing about which variable
to treat as the cause, or by how much $y$ changes when $x$ changes by one.

Regression answers both. It fits

$$\hat{y} = b_0 + b_1 x$$

where $b_1$ is the **slope** — how much $\hat{y}$ moves per one-unit increase in $x$
— and $b_0$ is the **intercept**, the fitted value when $x = 0$.

The asymmetry is the point. Regressing $y$ on $x$ is a different exercise from
regressing $x$ on $y$, while $r$ is the same either way.

## Choosing the line

Infinitely many lines pass through a cloud of points. The one we fit is the one that
makes the **residuals** as small as possible, where a residual is what the line
missed:

$$e_i = y_i - \hat{y}_i$$

Squaring them and adding gives a single number to minimise:

$$\text{SSE} = \sum (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2$$

Hence **least squares**. Squaring does the same two jobs as in Chapter 3: it removes
signs so positive and negative misses cannot cancel, and it penalises a big miss more
than several small ones.

Minimising gives formulas you already have the ingredients for:

$$b_1 = \frac{s_{xy}}{s_x^2} \qquad\qquad b_0 = \bar{y} - b_1\bar{x}$$

The slope is the covariance from Chapter 4 divided by the variance of $x$. And the
intercept formula rearranges to $\bar{y} = b_0 + b_1\bar{x}$ — **the line always
passes through the point of means**, which is a free check on your arithmetic.

## How well does it fit?

Total variation in $y$ splits in two, exactly as ANOVA's did:

$$\underbrace{\sum(y_i - \bar{y})^2}_{\text{SST}}
= \underbrace{\sum(\hat{y}_i - \bar{y})^2}_{\text{SSR, explained}}
+ \underbrace{\sum(y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}_{\text{SSE, left over}}$$

$$R^2 = \frac{\text{SSR}}{\text{SST}} = 1 - \frac{\text{SSE}}{\text{SST}}$$

**$R^2$ is the fraction of the variation in $y$ the line accounts for.** It runs from
0 to 1, and for a simple regression with one predictor it is exactly $r^2$ — so an
$r$ of $0.7$ means the line explains 49% of the variation, not 70%.

## Is the slope real?

$b_1$ is computed from a sample, so it is a statistic with a sampling distribution —
Chapter 11's idea once more. A different sample gives a different slope. So test it:

$$H_0: \beta_1 = 0 \qquad\text{against}\qquad H_a: \beta_1 \neq 0$$

$$t = \frac{b_1}{\text{SE}(b_1)} \qquad \nu = n - 2$$

$H_0$ says the slope is zero — that $x$ tells you nothing about $y$. Degrees of
freedom are $n - 2$ because two parameters were estimated from the data, $b_0$ and
$b_1$. Chapter 3's logic again: each estimate costs a degree of freedom.

## Worked example

Advertising spend $x$ (thousands) and sales $y$ (thousands of units):

| $x$ | 2 | 4 | 6 | 8 | 10 | 12 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| $y$ | 14 | 18 | 23 | 25 | 31 | 34 |

**(a) Fit the line. (b) Interpret the slope. (c) Find $R^2$. (d) Test the slope.**

### Solution

$\bar{x} = 7$ and $\bar{y} = 24.1667$.

**(a)** From the deviation table, $s_{xy} = 28.2$ and $s_x^2 = 14$, so

$$b_1 = \frac{28.2}{14} = 2.0143
\qquad
b_0 = 24.1667 - 2.0143(7) = 10.0667$$

$$\hat{y} = 10.0667 + 2.0143x$$

**(b)** Each extra **thousand dollars** of advertising is associated with about
**2,014 more units** sold. Note "associated with" — the data is observational, and
Chapter 4's warning has not expired.

**(c)** SSE $= 2.8190$ and SST $= 286.8333$, so

$$R^2 = 1 - \frac{2.8190}{286.8333} = 0.9902$$

The line accounts for **99%** of the variation in sales. Equivalently $r = 0.9951$,
and $0.9951^2 = 0.9902$.

**(d)** SE$(b_1) = 0.1003$, so

$$t = \frac{2.0143}{0.1003} = 20.0747 \qquad \nu = 4$$

Against $t_{0.025,4} = 2.7764$ this is overwhelming — $p = 0.00004$. **Reject
$H_0$**: the slope is not zero.

## What the line does not license

Three limits, and each has cost somebody money.

**Extrapolation.** The data runs from $x = 2$ to $x = 12$. Predicting at $x = 50$
assumes the straight line continues far beyond anything observed, and nothing in the
data supports that. Advertising returns almost certainly flatten.

**Causation.** A significant slope establishes association. Spend may drive sales;
sales may fund spend; or a growing market may drive both — Chapter 4's three
explanations, unchanged.

**The intercept is often meaningless.** Here $b_0 = 10.07$ is the predicted sales at
zero advertising, which is an extrapolation to a point outside the data. Report it
because the line needs it; interpret it only if $x = 0$ is genuinely in range.

## Checking the assumptions

Least squares assumes the relationship is **linear**, the residuals have **constant
variance** and are **independent**, and for the $t$ test that they are roughly
**normal**.

The check is a picture, not a number: plot the residuals against $\hat{y}$. You want
a formless band around zero. A curve means the relationship is not linear; a fan
widening to the right means the variance is not constant. Both are invisible in $R^2$,
which is why a high $R^2$ never substitutes for looking.

## In Stata

*The snippets below assume the header and preamble from
[Chapter 1](01_what_statistics_is_for.md).*

```stata
use "DATA/cps_small.dta", clear

scatter wage educ
regress wage educ

predict yhat
predict resid, residuals
scatter resid yhat, yline(0)
```

`regress` prints the coefficients, their standard errors, the $t$ statistics and
$p$-values, $R^2$, and the ANOVA table splitting SST into model and residual — the
same decomposition as Chapter 16.

The last three lines are the assumption check, and they matter more than anything in
the output table.

## In Python

```python
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats

x = np.array([2, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12])
y = np.array([14, 18, 23, 25, 31, 34])

fit = stats.linregress(x, y)
print(fit.slope, fit.intercept)       # 2.0143   10.0667
print(fit.rvalue**2)                  # 0.9902
print(fit.stderr, fit.pvalue)         # 0.1003   0.00004
```

`linregress` returns everything in one object. Note `rvalue` is $r$, so square it for
$R^2$.

To plot the fit and then the residuals:

```python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

plt.scatter(x, y, color="#8b1a1a")
plt.plot(x, fit.intercept + fit.slope*x, color="#222222")
plt.show()

resid = y - (fit.intercept + fit.slope*x)
plt.scatter(fit.intercept + fit.slope*x, resid)
plt.axhline(0, color="#999999")
plt.show()
```

## What goes wrong

**Extrapolating.** The line is evidence only across the range of $x$ you observed.

**Reading causation into a slope.** It is association, however small the $p$-value.

**Reporting $R^2$ as a percentage of $r$.** $r = 0.7$ gives $R^2 = 0.49$, not 0.70.

**Interpreting $b_0$ when $x = 0$ is outside the data.**

**Using $n-1$ degrees of freedom.** It is $n - 2$ for simple regression: two
parameters were estimated.

**Trusting a high $R^2$ without plotting the residuals.** A curved relationship can
produce an impressive $R^2$ and a systematically wrong line.

**Regressing $x$ on $y$ when you meant $y$ on $x$.** The two give different slopes.

## Exercises

1. For $n = 10$ pairs, $s_{xy} = 24$, $s_x^2 = 16$, $\bar{x} = 5$, $\bar{y} = 30$.
   (a) Find $b_1$ and $b_0$.
   (b) Write the fitted line and predict $y$ at $x = 8$.

2. A regression gives SST $= 500$ and SSE $= 125$.
   (a) Find SSR and $R^2$.
   (b) What is $r$, and can you determine its sign?

3. A fitted line is $\hat{y} = 45 - 3.2x$, from data where $x$ ranged from 5 to 20.
   (a) Interpret the slope.
   (b) Would you use it to predict at $x = 40$? Explain.
   (c) Is the intercept meaningful here?

4. A slope is $b_1 = 1.8$ with SE$(b_1) = 0.9$, from $n = 12$ observations.
   (a) Compute $t$ and the degrees of freedom.
   (b) Test at $\alpha = 0.05$, given $t_{0.025,10} = 2.2281$.
   (c) What does your conclusion mean in words?

5. A residual plot shows a clear U shape. What has gone wrong, and would $R^2$ reveal
   it?

### Answers

1. (a) $b_1 = 24/16 = 1.5$; $b_0 = 30 - 1.5(5) = 22.5$.
   (b) $\hat{y} = 22.5 + 1.5x$, so at $x = 8$, $\hat{y} = 34.5$.

2. (a) SSR $= 500 - 125 = 375$, so $R^2 = 375/500 = 0.75$.
   (b) $r = \pm\sqrt{0.75} = \pm 0.8660$. **The sign cannot be determined from $R^2$**
   — you need the sign of the slope or the covariance.

3. (a) Each one-unit increase in $x$ is associated with a decrease of 3.2 in $\hat{y}$.
   (b) **No.** 40 is double the largest observed $x$; the linear relationship is
   evidence only within 5 to 20.
   (c) **No.** $x = 0$ lies outside the observed range, so 45 is an extrapolation.

4. (a) $t = 1.8/0.9 = 2.00$ with $\nu = 12 - 2 = 10$.
   (b) $2.00 < 2.2281$, so **do not reject**.
   (c) There is insufficient evidence that the slope differs from zero — this sample
